Well, it's... I mean, work is definitely a part of it, but I wouldn't say it's just one thing. It's more like... my mind just doesn't stop. I hope I'm not overcomplicating this, but it just kind of jumps around.

Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like I'm falling apart or anything. I mean, I'm 55, so some of it is probably just getting older, right? And I really try to keep my energy up for the students. 

But... I guess I have been feeling pretty tired. More than usual, maybe. By the time the last bell rings, I just feel... wrung out. And it's not the kind of tired that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday seems to fix anymore. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes even just grading a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud. 

I'm sure it's nothing major, and I probably just need to manage my time better so I can rest more. I hope I'm not complaining too much... is that the kind of thing you were looking for?

Well, I... I try really hard to keep a handle on them. I mean, I know I should be able to just tell myself to stop and move on. And sometimes I can, for a little bit. 

But... if I'm being completely honest, once a worry really takes hold, I don't feel like I have much control at all. It's like... like a snowball rolling downhill. I try to catch it, but it just keeps getting bigger and faster. I really wish I could just snap out of it, and I feel awful that I can't. I guess what finally made me come in is just this physical tension. \n\nI'm sure it's probably just normal stress from teaching, and I feel a little silly complaining about it, but my neck, my shoulders, and even my jaw are just tight almost all the time now. It's like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, you know? Even when I'm just sitting at my desk grading papers, or trying to watch TV at night. I just can't seem to unclench. \n\nDoes that... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a number of different things, or is your tension mostly related to specific stressors like work or events in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, work is definitely a part of it, but I wouldn't say it's just one thing. It's more like... my mind just doesn't stop. And then... well, I guess the biggest thing is I just worry I'm disappointing my wife and my family because I can't seem to just keep everything under control. \n\nI know it sounds like I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really should be able to handle it better. I suppose they are. I mean, I really try not to let it get to me, and I don't want to sound like I'm completely incapable of doing my job or anything. I'm still getting to my classes and doing my job, so it's not disabling me or anything like that... I probably just need to learn how to push through it better. I hope that makes sense? And I do try, I really want to just be present for the kids. But then... just one little thought will slip in. Like, um, maybe remembering a late invoice for the house, or noticing that a student is falling behind. And instead of just making a note of it, it sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. \n\nOnce it starts, I just... I can't seem to shut it down. I feel a bit embarrassed admitting that, to be honest. A grown man should be able to just compartmentalize and brush it off, right? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information here... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms, like stomach issues, headaches, or fatigue, that don’t seem to have a clear medical cause?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like I'm falling apart or anything. I mean, I'm 55, so some of it is probably just getting older, right? And I really try to keep my energy up for the students. \n\nBut... I guess I have been feeling pretty tired. More than usual, maybe. By the time the last bell rings, I just feel... wrung out. And it's not the kind of tired that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday seems to fix anymore. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes even just grading a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud. \n\nI'm sure it's nothing major, and I probably just need to manage my time better so I can rest more. I hope I'm not complaining too much... is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: How much control do you feel you have over these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try really hard to keep a handle on them. I mean, I know I should be able to just tell myself to stop and move on. It's like... like a snowball rolling downhill. I try to catch it, but it just keeps getting bigger and faster. I really wish I could just snap out of it, and I feel awful that I can't. I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds weak, a guy my age not being able to control his own thoughts. Am I making sense? I haven't really been to the doctor for it. I mean, I had my regular check-up a while back, and everything seemed fine then. I wouldn't say that. I mean, everyone gets tired, right? Especially in teaching. I'm sure most of my colleagues are dealing with the exact same aches and exhaustion. I really don't want to act like my situation is somehow worse or more special than anyone else's. \n\nIf anything, I feel a little guilty for even bringing it up, because I'm sure other people handle it much better than I do. I'm still getting to my classes and doing my job, so it's not disabling me or anything like that... I probably just need to learn how to push through it better. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want to come across as a complainer when so many people deal with much worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep or staying asleep because your mind is racing or you’re unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say my mind is racing out of control or anything... maybe just going over the lesson plans for the next day or thinking about a student who's struggling. Just typical teacher stuff, I'm sure. I definitely don't want to make it sound like I'm an insomniac or anything dramatic like that. I usually manage to get enough rest to function the next day. I mean, work is definitely a part of it, but I wouldn't say it's just one thing. It's more like... my mind just doesn't stop. I hope I'm not overcomplicating this, but it just kind of jumps around. \n\nLike, we're having some work done on the house, and I'll start thinking, what if the contractors missed something important? And then, before I know it, I'm worrying about my lesson plans, like maybe I'm letting things slip there. Or I'll stress about the kids in the robotics club I run, worrying they're falling behind. And then... well, I guess the biggest thing is I just worry I'm disappointing my wife and my family because I can't seem to just keep everything under control. \n\nI know it sounds like I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, and I really should be able to handle it better. Is it normal to let all these little things pile up like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are constant, even when there isn't a clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose they are. I mean, I really try not to let it get to me, and I don't want to sound like I'm completely incapable of doing my job or anything. But... yes, it does feel pretty constant. \n\nI'll be at work, and I can tell myself to just stop worrying and focus on the class in front of me. And I do try, I really want to just be present for the kids. But then... just one little thought will slip in. Like, um, maybe remembering a late invoice for the house, or noticing that a student is falling behind. And instead of just making a note of it, it sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. \n\nOnce it starts, I just... I can't seem to shut it down. I feel a bit embarrassed admitting that, to be honest. A grown man should be able to just compartmentalize and brush it off, right? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information here... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical symptoms, like stomach issues, headaches, or fatigue, that don’t seem to have a clear medical cause?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like I'm falling apart or anything. I mean, I'm 55, so some of it is probably just getting older, right? And I really try to keep my energy up for the students. \n\nBut... I guess I have been feeling pretty tired. More than usual, maybe. By the time the last bell rings, I just feel... wrung out. And it's not the kind of tired that a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday seems to fix anymore. It's embarrassing to admit, but sometimes even just grading a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud. \n\nI'm sure it's nothing major, and I probably just need to manage my time better so I can rest more. I hope I'm not complaining too much... is that the kind of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: How much control do you feel you have over these worries once they start?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try really hard to keep a handle on them. I mean, I know I should be able to just tell myself to stop and move on. And sometimes I can, for a little bit. \n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest, once a worry really takes hold, I don't feel like I have much control at all. It's like... like a snowball rolling downhill. I try to catch it, but it just keeps getting bigger and faster. I really wish I could just snap out of it, and I feel awful that I can't. I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds weak, a guy my age not being able to control his own thoughts. Am I making sense? I hope I'm answering your question properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen a doctor or had any tests done recently to check if there’s a medical reason for your physical tension and fatigue?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I haven't really been to the doctor for it. I mean, I had my regular check-up a while back, and everything seemed fine then. I really didn't want to bother anyone or take up an appointment just because I'm feeling a little stiff and tired, you know? \n\nI figured it was just the stress of the school year and, well, being 55. I didn't want my doctor to think I was being a hypochondriac or making a fuss over nothing. I probably should have gone in, shouldn't I? I hope it's okay that I haven't had any specific tests done for this... does that mess up what we're trying to figure out here?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your physical symptoms, like the tension and fatigue, are more distressing or disabling than a typical person would experience under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I... I wouldn't say that. I mean, everyone gets tired, right? Especially in teaching. I'm sure most of my colleagues are dealing with the exact same aches and exhaustion. I really don't want to act like my situation is somehow worse or more special than anyone else's. \n\nIf anything, I feel a little guilty for even bringing it up, because I'm sure other people handle it much better than I do. I'm still getting to my classes and doing my job, so it's not disabling me or anything like that... I probably just need to learn how to push through it better. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want to come across as a complainer when so many people deal with much worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep or staying asleep because your mind is racing or you’re unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I do toss and turn a bit sometimes, but I think that's pretty normal, right? I wouldn't say my mind is racing out of control or anything... maybe just going over the lesson plans for the next day or thinking about a student who's struggling. Just typical teacher stuff, I'm sure. I definitely don't want to make it sound like I'm an insomniac or anything dramatic like that. I usually manage to get enough rest to function the next day. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not giving you the wrong idea.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself, like you're observing your own thoughts or body from outside, or felt like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt anything like that. That sounds... well, a bit frightening, actually. I mean, I'm always very present, very much just... me, doing my job and living my life. \n\nI definitely don't feel like I'm outside my body or that things are unreal. I try to stay very grounded, you know? I wouldn't want you to think I'm losing touch with reality or anything. I'm just a bit stressed, that's all. I hope it's okay that I don't have that symptom... I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're watching yourself from the outside or that the world around you feels distant or foggy, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think that's a little like what you just asked a moment ago, right? But, no, I... I still haven't ever felt anything like that. No fogginess or feeling distant. \n\nI really have to stay pretty sharp and focused for my students, you know? Well, um... I guess I'd say there's usually a reason for it. I mean, life is just naturally busy, isn't it? There's always something going on.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh... I feel terrible, I know you're trying to get to the bottom of this, but... I feel like I'm repeating myself, and I really don't mean to be a difficult patient. 

It's just... no, not really. I mean, sure, my neck gets tight sometimes, but who doesn't get a stiff neck when they've been hunched over a desk grading papers for three hours? It's just the physical toll of the job, you know? I wouldn't say it happens out of the blue when everything is perfectly fine. If I'm tense, it's because there's a stack of work on my desk that needs my attention. 

I'm a very practical person, I really am. I don't just sit around letting my body get all knotted up over nothing. I hope that's the kind of answer you're looking for? I'm so sorry if I'm not giving you the right examples... I really am trying my best to be helpful.

I hope it's okay that I haven't had any specific tests done for this... does that mess up what we're trying to figure out here?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your physical symptoms, like the tension and fatigue, are more distressing or disabling than a typical person would experience under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I... I wouldn't say that. I mean, everyone gets tired, right? Especially in teaching. I'm sure most of my colleagues are dealing with the exact same aches and exhaustion. I really don't want to act like my situation is somehow worse or more special than anyone else's. \n\nIf anything, I feel a little guilty for even bringing it up, because I'm sure other people handle it much better than I do. I'm still getting to my classes and doing my job, so it's not disabling me or anything like that... I probably just need to learn how to push through it better. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want to come across as a complainer when so many people deal with much worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep or staying asleep because your mind is racing or you’re unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I do toss and turn a bit sometimes, but I think that's pretty normal, right? I wouldn't say my mind is racing out of control or anything... maybe just going over the lesson plans for the next day or thinking about a student who's struggling. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not giving you the wrong idea.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself, like you're observing your own thoughts or body from outside, or felt like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt anything like that. I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're watching yourself from the outside or that the world around you feels distant or foggy, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think that's a little like what you just asked a moment ago, right? But, no, I... I still haven't ever felt anything like that. No fogginess or feeling distant. \n\nI really have to stay pretty sharp and focused for my students, you know? So, no, definitely nothing like watching myself from the outside. \n\nI'm sorry if I'm not giving you much to go on with these particular questions... I really do want to be as helpful as possible for you. Is it... is it okay that I don't have that happening? I really didn't want to bother anyone or take up an appointment just because I'm feeling a little stiff and tired, you know? \n\nI figured it was just the stress of the school year and, well, being 55. I didn't want my doctor to think I was being a hypochondriac or making a fuss over nothing. I probably should have gone in, shouldn't I? I hope it's okay that I haven't had any specific tests done for this... does that mess up what we're trying to figure out here?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your physical symptoms, like the tension and fatigue, are more distressing or disabling than a typical person would experience under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I... I wouldn't say that. I mean, everyone gets tired, right? Especially in teaching. I'm sure most of my colleagues are dealing with the exact same aches and exhaustion. I really don't want to act like my situation is somehow worse or more special than anyone else's. \n\nIf anything, I feel a little guilty for even bringing it up, because I'm sure other people handle it much better than I do. I'm still getting to my classes and doing my job, so it's not disabling me or anything like that... I probably just need to learn how to push through it better. I hope that makes sense? I really don't want to come across as a complainer when so many people deal with much worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep or staying asleep because your mind is racing or you’re unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I do toss and turn a bit sometimes, but I think that's pretty normal, right? I mean, who doesn't have a few restless nights here and there, especially during grading season? \n\nI really try to just close my eyes and focus on resting, even if I'm not fully asleep. I wouldn't say my mind is racing out of control or anything... maybe just going over the lesson plans for the next day or thinking about a student who's struggling. Just typical teacher stuff, I'm sure. I definitely don't want to make it sound like I'm an insomniac or anything dramatic like that. I usually manage to get enough rest to function the next day. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not giving you the wrong idea.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself, like you're observing your own thoughts or body from outside, or felt like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never felt anything like that. That sounds... well, a bit frightening, actually. I mean, I'm always very present, very much just... me, doing my job and living my life. \n\nI definitely don't feel like I'm outside my body or that things are unreal. I try to stay very grounded, you know? I wouldn't want you to think I'm losing touch with reality or anything. I'm just a bit stressed, that's all. I hope it's okay that I don't have that symptom... I know you're trying to be thorough, and I appreciate that, I really do. Did I answer that right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're watching yourself from the outside or that the world around you feels distant or foggy, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think that's a little like what you just asked a moment ago, right? But, no, I... I still haven't ever felt anything like that. No fogginess or feeling distant. \n\nI really have to stay pretty sharp and focused for my students, you know? So, no, definitely nothing like watching myself from the outside. \n\nI'm sorry if I'm not giving you much to go on with these particular questions... I really do want to be as helpful as possible for you. Is it... is it okay that I don't have that happening? I hope I'm still giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety and tension continue even when there are no specific stressors or obvious reasons for worry present in your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um...
